========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I guess the main reason is that people keep telling me I’m “overthinking” something, and I don’t think I am. It’s about my father’s death, and what happened around it. I’ve been trying to put the pieces together—hospital records, donation emails, the timing of certain messages—and everyone keeps saying it all lines up, that there’s nothing unusual there. But when I actually compare the timestamps, and then I see attachments that should be there but aren’t, and then certain people in the community suddenly avoid answering basic questions… I mean, to me, that doesn’t feel like coincidence.

I’m a systems analyst, so I’m used to looking at logs and patterns and whether things match. And in this case, they don’t match cleanly. People act like I’m being difficult just because I’m asking for the full picture, but it seems obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened. I don’t know exactly who is responsible for all of it, but I know there are gaps, and the way people react when I bring them up makes it feel even more clear that there’s something they don’t want me to see.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It started after he died—more specifically, in the period afterward when I was going through his records and messages. So, not like years and years ago or something I’ve always thought about everything. I mean, I’ve always been detail-oriented because of my work, but this particular certainty, the sense that someone is hiding the real story, has been going on for about nine months.

At first it was just… I was sorting things out, trying to make sense of what had happened, and I noticed a few things that didn’t sit right. Then the more I looked, the more it felt like the gaps weren’t just normal missing information. And over those months it became harder for me to see it as anything else. So I’d say about nine months, since I started really digging into his records and messages after his death.

The conviction is fixed and not experienced as intrusive/unwanted, making OCD (F42) less likely, so it is removed. I don’t know that I’d put it that way generally. I mean, I’m not walking around thinking every person I deal with is trying to take advantage of me, if that’s what you mean. Can you start by telling me what's been bringing you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess the main reason is that people keep telling me I’m “overthinking” something, and I don’t think I am. It’s about my father’s death, and what happened around it. I’ve been trying to put the pieces together—hospital records, donation emails, the timing of certain messages—and everyone keeps saying it all lines up, that there’s nothing unusual there. But when I actually compare the timestamps, and then I see attachments that should be there but aren’t, and then certain people in the community suddenly avoid answering basic questions… I mean, to me, that doesn’t feel like coincidence.\n\nI’m a systems analyst, so I’m used to looking at logs and patterns and whether things match. And in this case, they don’t match cleanly. People act like I’m being difficult just because I’m asking for the full picture, but it seems obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened. I don’t know exactly who is responsible for all of it, but I know there are gaps, and the way people react when I bring them up makes it feel even more clear that there’s something they don’t want me to see.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this certainty that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death—did it start right around the time he died, or has this kind of thinking been part of how you see things for a longer time, even before he passed?\n\nPatient: It started after he died—more specifically, in the period afterward when I was going through his records and messages. So, not like years and years ago or something I’ve always thought about everything. I mean, I’ve always been detail-oriented because of my work, but this particular certainty, the sense that someone is hiding the real story, has been going on for about nine months.\n\nAt first it was just… I was sorting things out, trying to make sense of what had happened, and I noticed a few things that didn’t sit right. Then the more I looked, the more it felt like the gaps weren’t just normal missing information. And over those months it became harder for me to see it as anything else. So I’d say about nine months, since I started really digging into his records and messages after his death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation with your father's death, do you generally find yourself suspicious of other people's motives or worried that others might be trying to deceive or exploit you in your everyday life and relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way generally. I mean, I’m not walking around thinking every person I deal with is trying to take advantage of me, if that’s what you mean. Like at work, if someone misses a deadline or gives me incomplete information, I usually assume it’s normal incompetence or miscommunication before I assume it’s malicious.\n\nI can be cautious, sure. I think most people are, especially if something doesn’t add up. But outside of this situation with my father, I haven’t really noticed myself constantly questioning everyone’s motives. It’s more that this particular set of facts feels… different. The reactions, the missing pieces, the way people shut down when I ask. That’s what bothers me. I’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf someone showed you a clear, verifiable explanation for one of these gaps—something that fully accounted for it without any wrongdoing—do you think that would change your mind about it, or would you still feel that something was being hidden?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, but when I compare the timestamps, missing attachments, and the way certain community members avoid my questions, it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… if it were actually clear and verifiable, I’d look at it. I’m not trying to reject reality just to hold onto a theory. That’s the part people don’t seem to understand—I work with systems, so if a log explains something, fine, then it explains it.

But the problem is that every time someone says, “Oh, that lines up,” it doesn’t really line up when I compare the details myself. The hospital records, the donation emails, the timestamps—there are these little mismatches. And then there are attachments that should be there but aren’t, or people in the community who suddenly get very evasive when I ask something basic. So if you showed me one explanation for one gap, okay, maybe that one gap could be explained. But that wouldn’t automatically explain all the other things around it.

So… I guess yes, it could change my mind about that specific piece. But honestly, with the whole pattern, I’d still have a hard time believing nothing was being hidden. It’s just too consistent to ignore.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure how to separate that cleanly, honestly. I mean, he was my father, so of course there have been moments where I feel sad about it, and I’ve had times where I just kind of sit there thinking about him or about what doesn’t make sense. But I don’t know that I’d describe it as this constant sadness that’s the main thing every day.

The sleep thing is hard to answer too, because if I’m up late, it’s usually because I’m going back through something or trying to line up details in my head, not just lying there feeling depressed, if that distinction matters. And with interest in things… I still do things, I still work, I still function. It’s more that this whole situation takes up a lot of mental space.

So, I guess my answer is: I’ve had normal grief, or what feels normal to me, but I haven’t really thought of it as a separate ongoing mood problem apart from trying to understand what happened.

No, not really. I mean, I don’t hear voices or see people who aren’t there or anything like that. I haven’t had some… presence in the room or messages coming through the walls or whatever people sometimes mean by that.

What happens is more ordinary, I think. I’ll notice patterns, or I’ll remember something someone said, or I’ll get stuck on why a document doesn’t match another document. But that’s me thinking about it, not something I’m perceiving that other people couldn’t perceive if they looked at the same material.

Sometimes I’ll get a feeling that something is off, but that’s not like sensing a ghost or hearing my father or anything. It’s more that the facts don’t sit right. So, unless you mean something broader by “sensing presences,” I’d say no, I haven’t experienced that.

But when I actually compare the timestamps, and then I see attachments that should be there but aren’t, and then certain people in the community suddenly avoid answering basic questions… I mean, to me, that doesn’t feel like coincidence.\n\nI’m a systems analyst, so I’m used to looking at logs and patterns and whether things match. And in this case, they don’t match cleanly. People act like I’m being difficult just because I’m asking for the full picture, but it seems obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened. I don’t know exactly who is responsible for all of it, but I know there are gaps, and the way people react when I bring them up makes it feel even more clear that there’s something they don’t want me to see.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this certainty that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death—did it start right around the time he died, or has this kind of thinking been part of how you see things for a longer time, even before he passed?\n\nPatient: It started after he died—more specifically, in the period afterward when I was going through his records and messages. So, not like years and years ago or something I’ve always thought about everything. I mean, I’ve always been detail-oriented because of my work, but this particular certainty, the sense that someone is hiding the real story, has been going on for about nine months.\n\nAt first it was just… I was sorting things out, trying to make sense of what had happened, and I noticed a few things that didn’t sit right. Then the more I looked, the more it felt like the gaps weren’t just normal missing information. And over those months it became harder for me to see it as anything else. So I’d say about nine months, since I started really digging into his records and messages after his death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation with your father's death, do you generally find yourself suspicious of other people's motives or worried that others might be trying to deceive or exploit you in your everyday life and relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way generally. I mean, I’m not walking around thinking every person I deal with is trying to take advantage of me, if that’s what you mean. Like at work, if someone misses a deadline or gives me incomplete information, I usually assume it’s normal incompetence or miscommunication before I assume it’s malicious.\n\nI can be cautious, sure. I think most people are, especially if something doesn’t add up. But outside of this situation with my father, I haven’t really noticed myself constantly questioning everyone’s motives. It’s more that this particular set of facts feels… different. The reactions, the missing pieces, the way people shut down when I ask. That’s what bothers me. I’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you a clear, verifiable explanation for one of these gaps—something that fully accounted for it without any wrongdoing—do you think that would change your mind about it, or would you still feel that something was being hidden?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it were actually clear and verifiable, I’d look at it. I’m not trying to reject reality just to hold onto a theory. That’s the part people don’t seem to understand—I work with systems, so if a log explains something, fine, then it explains it.\n\nBut the problem is that every time someone says, “Oh, that lines up,” it doesn’t really line up when I compare the details myself. The hospital records, the donation emails, the timestamps—there are these little mismatches. And then there are attachments that should be there but aren’t, or people in the community who suddenly get very evasive when I ask something basic. So if you showed me one explanation for one gap, okay, maybe that one gap could be explained. But that wouldn’t automatically explain all the other things around it.\n\nSo… I guess yes, it could change my mind about that specific piece. But honestly, with the whole pattern, I’d still have a hard time believing nothing was being hidden. It’s just too consistent to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Since your father passed, have you also been dealing with things like ongoing sadness, crying spells, trouble sleeping, or loss of interest in things you used to enjoy—apart from the concerns about what happened to him?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that cleanly, honestly. I mean, he was my father, so of course there have been moments where I feel sad about it, and I’ve had times where I just kind of sit there thinking about him or about what doesn’t make sense. But I don’t know that I’d describe it as this constant sadness that’s the main thing every day.\n\nThe sleep thing is hard to answer too, because if I’m up late, it’s usually because I’m going back through something or trying to line up details in my head, not just lying there feeling depressed, if that distinction matters. And with interest in things… I still do things, I still work, I still function. It’s more that this whole situation takes up a lot of mental space.\n\nSo, I guess my answer is: I’ve had normal grief, or what feels normal to me, but I haven’t really thought of it as a separate ongoing mood problem apart from trying to understand what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, seeing things, or sensing presences that others don't perceive, related to this situation or otherwise?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don’t hear voices or see people who aren’t there or anything like that. I haven’t had some… presence in the room or messages coming through the walls or whatever people sometimes mean by that.\n\nWhat happens is more ordinary, I think. I’ll notice patterns, or I’ll remember something someone said, or I’ll get stuck on why a document doesn’t match another document. But that’s me thinking about it, not something I’m perceiving that other people couldn’t perceive if they looked at the same material.\n\nSometimes I’ll get a feeling that something is off, but that’s not like sensing a ghost or hearing my father or anything. It’s more that the facts don’t sit right. So, unless you mean something broader by “sensing presences,” I’d say no, I haven’t experienced that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’d say most of the normal structure of my life is still intact. I still go to work, I still handle my projects, I pay my bills on time, I keep up with the usual routine—laundry, groceries, emails, all of that. I don’t think anyone at work would say I’ve stopped functioning or anything like that. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do.

Where it gets affected is more… socially, I guess, or with certain people. If I’m just talking about normal everyday things, it’s fine. But when I bring up questions about my father’s records—like something that doesn’t match, or something I think someone should be able to explain—then things get tense. Especially with family, or people in the community who were involved or around at the time. They’ll shut down, or act like I’m being unreasonable for even asking, and then the whole conversation changes.

So no, my whole life hasn’t fallen apart. It’s not like I can’t get out of bed or do my job. But this one area has definitely put strain on relationships, because I don’t feel like I can just pretend the questions aren’t there.

But when I actually compare the timestamps, and then I see attachments that should be there but aren’t, and then certain people in the community suddenly avoid answering basic questions… I mean, to me, that doesn’t feel like coincidence.\n\nI’m a systems analyst, so I’m used to looking at logs and patterns and whether things match. And in this case, they don’t match cleanly. People act like I’m being difficult just because I’m asking for the full picture, but it seems obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened. I don’t know exactly who is responsible for all of it, but I know there are gaps, and the way people react when I bring them up makes it feel even more clear that there’s something they don’t want me to see.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this certainty that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death—did it start right around the time he died, or has this kind of thinking been part of how you see things for a longer time, even before he passed?\n\nPatient: It started after he died—more specifically, in the period afterward when I was going through his records and messages. So, not like years and years ago or something I’ve always thought about everything. I mean, I’ve always been detail-oriented because of my work, but this particular certainty, the sense that someone is hiding the real story, has been going on for about nine months.\n\nAt first it was just… I was sorting things out, trying to make sense of what had happened, and I noticed a few things that didn’t sit right. Then the more I looked, the more it felt like the gaps weren’t just normal missing information. And over those months it became harder for me to see it as anything else. So I’d say about nine months, since I started really digging into his records and messages after his death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation with your father's death, do you generally find yourself suspicious of other people's motives or worried that others might be trying to deceive or exploit you in your everyday life and relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way generally. I mean, I’m not walking around thinking every person I deal with is trying to take advantage of me, if that’s what you mean. Like at work, if someone misses a deadline or gives me incomplete information, I usually assume it’s normal incompetence or miscommunication before I assume it’s malicious.\n\nI can be cautious, sure. I think most people are, especially if something doesn’t add up. But outside of this situation with my father, I haven’t really noticed myself constantly questioning everyone’s motives. It’s more that this particular set of facts feels… different. The reactions, the missing pieces, the way people shut down when I ask. That’s what bothers me. I’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you a clear, verifiable explanation for one of these gaps—something that fully accounted for it without any wrongdoing—do you think that would change your mind about it, or would you still feel that something was being hidden?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it were actually clear and verifiable, I’d look at it. I’m not trying to reject reality just to hold onto a theory. That’s the part people don’t seem to understand—I work with systems, so if a log explains something, fine, then it explains it.\n\nBut the problem is that every time someone says, “Oh, that lines up,” it doesn’t really line up when I compare the details myself. The hospital records, the donation emails, the timestamps—there are these little mismatches. And then there are attachments that should be there but aren’t, or people in the community who suddenly get very evasive when I ask something basic. So if you showed me one explanation for one gap, okay, maybe that one gap could be explained. But that wouldn’t automatically explain all the other things around it.\n\nSo… I guess yes, it could change my mind about that specific piece. But honestly, with the whole pattern, I’d still have a hard time believing nothing was being hidden. It’s just too consistent to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Since your father passed, have you also been dealing with things like ongoing sadness, crying spells, trouble sleeping, or loss of interest in things you used to enjoy—apart from the concerns about what happened to him?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that cleanly, honestly. I mean, he was my father, so of course there have been moments where I feel sad about it, and I’ve had times where I just kind of sit there thinking about him or about what doesn’t make sense. But I don’t know that I’d describe it as this constant sadness that’s the main thing every day.\n\nThe sleep thing is hard to answer too, because if I’m up late, it’s usually because I’m going back through something or trying to line up details in my head, not just lying there feeling depressed, if that distinction matters. And with interest in things… I still do things, I still work, I still function. It’s more that this whole situation takes up a lot of mental space.\n\nSo, I guess my answer is: I’ve had normal grief, or what feels normal to me, but I haven’t really thought of it as a separate ongoing mood problem apart from trying to understand what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, seeing things, or sensing presences that others don't perceive, related to this situation or otherwise?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don’t hear voices or see people who aren’t there or anything like that. I haven’t had some… presence in the room or messages coming through the walls or whatever people sometimes mean by that.\n\nWhat happens is more ordinary, I think. I’ll notice patterns, or I’ll remember something someone said, or I’ll get stuck on why a document doesn’t match another document. But that’s me thinking about it, not something I’m perceiving that other people couldn’t perceive if they looked at the same material.\n\nSometimes I’ll get a feeling that something is off, but that’s not like sensing a ghost or hearing my father or anything. It’s more that the facts don’t sit right. So, unless you mean something broader by “sensing presences,” I’d say no, I haven’t experienced that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this investigation into your father's death, has your day-to-day functioning—like your work performance, relationships, or ability to concentrate on other tasks—been affected, or does everything else in your life feel pretty much the same as before this started?\n\nPatient: I’d say most of the normal structure of my life is still intact. I still go to work, I still handle my projects, I pay my bills on time, I keep up with the usual routine—laundry, groceries, emails, all of that. I don’t think anyone at work would say I’ve stopped functioning or anything like that. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nWhere it gets affected is more… socially, I guess, or with certain people. If I’m just talking about normal everyday things, it’s fine. But when I bring up questions about my father’s records—like something that doesn’t match, or something I think someone should be able to explain—then things get tense. Especially with family, or people in the community who were involved or around at the time. They’ll shut down, or act like I’m being unreasonable for even asking, and then the whole conversation changes.\n\nSo no, my whole life hasn’t fallen apart. It’s not like I can’t get out of bed or do my job. But this one area has definitely put strain on relationships, because I don’t feel like I can just pretend the questions aren’t there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.  Subtypes: Persecutory Delusions, Referential Delusions, Grandiose Delusions, Erotomanic Delusions, Nihilistic Delusions, Somatic Delusions, Jealous Delusions\n[S020] Hallucinations: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t know that I’d put it that way generally. I mean, I’m not walking around thinking every person I deal with is trying to take advantage of me, if that’s what you mean. Like at work, if someone misses a deadline or gives me incomplete information, I usually assume it’s normal incompetence or miscommunication before I assume it’s malicious.\n\nI can be cautious, sure. I think most people are, especially if something doesn’t add up. But outside of this situation with my father, I haven’t really noticed myself constantly questioning everyone’s motives. It’s more that this particular set of facts feels… different. The reactions, the missing pieces, the way people shut down when I ask. That’s what bothers me. I’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. The hospital records, the donation emails, the timestamps—there are these little mismatches. So if you showed me one explanation for one gap, okay, maybe that one gap could be explained. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, not really. I mean, I don’t hear voices or see people who aren’t there or anything like that. I haven’t had some… presence in the room or messages coming through the walls or whatever people sometimes mean by that.\n\nWhat happens is more ordinary, I think. I’ll notice patterns, or I’ll remember something someone said, or I’ll get stuck on why a document doesn’t match another document. But that’s me thinking about it, not something I’m perceiving that other people couldn’t perceive if they looked at the same material.\n\nSometimes I’ll get a feeling that something is off, but that’s not like sensing a ghost or hearing my father or anything. It’s more that the facts don’t sit right. So, unless you mean something broader by “sensing presences,” I’d say no, I haven’t experienced that.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
